Cognacy Queries over Dependence Graphs for Transparent Visualisations
Joe Bond, Cristina David, Minh Nguyen, Dominic Orchard, Roly Perera

Abstract
Charts, figures, and text derived from data play an important role in decision making, from data-driven policy development to day-to-day choices informed by online articles. Making sense of or fact-checking outputs means understanding how they relate to the underlying data. Even for domain experts with access to the source code and data sets, this poses a significant challenge. In this paper we introduce a new program analysis framework which supports interactive exploration of fine-grained IO relationships directly through computed outputs, making use of dynamic dependence graphs. This framework enables a novel notion in data provenance which we call linked inputs , a relation of mutual relevance or ‘‘cognacy’’ which arises between inputs when they contribute to common features of the output. Queries of this form allow readers to ask questions like ‘‘What outputs use this data element, and what other data elements are used along with it?’’. We show how [ 21 ] ’s concept of conjugate operators on Boolean algebras appropriately characterises the notion of cognacy in a dependence graph, and give a procedure for computing linked inputs over such a graph. To demonstrate the approach in practice, we present a functional programming language called [OurLanguage] which automatically enriches visual outputs with interactions supporting linked inputs and similar fine-grained queries. We show how to obtain more informative, contextualised queries via projection operators which factor out data contributed by specific inputs or demand associated with specific outputs. We also show how to derive a linked outputs operator, capturing a dual cognacy relation between output features whenever they compete for common input data. Composed with suitable projections, this recovers a prior approach to linked outputs based on execution traces and a bidirectional interpreter. However, the dependence graph approach presented in this paper performs better (computationally) on most examples than an implementation based on execution traces and is significantly simpler to implement.



1

Introduction: Towards Transparent Research Outputs

Whether formulating national policy or making day-to-day decisions about our own lives, we increasingly rely
on the charts, figures and text created by scientists and journalists. Interpreting these visual and textual
summaries is essential to making informed decisions. However, most of the artefacts we encounter are

Figure 1
:
A hand-crafted transparent visualisation due to
[
5
]

opaque
, inasmuch as they are unable to reveal anything about how they relate to the data they were
derived from. Whilst one could in principle try to use the source code and data to reverse engineer some of
these relationships, this requires substantial expertise, as well as valuable time spent away from the
‘‘comprehension context’’ in which we encountered the output in question. These difficulties are compounded
when the information presented draws on multiple data sources, such as medical
meta-analyses
[
12
]
, ensemble models in climate science
[
24
]
, or queries that span
multiple database tables
[
35
]
. Even professional reviewers may lack the resources or inclination
to embark on such an activity. Perhaps more often than we would like, we end up taking things on trust.

With traditional print media, a ‘‘disconnect’’ between outputs and underlying data is unavoidable. For digital
media, other options are open to us. One way to improve things is to engineer visual artefacts to be more
‘‘self-explanatory’’ so they can reveal the relationship to the underlying data to an interested user.
Consider the histogram in Figure
1
showing urban population growth in Asia over a 10-year
period
[
5
]
. There are many questions a reader might have about what this chart represents --- in
other words, how visual elements map to underlying data. Do the points represent individual cities? What does
the colour scheme indicate? Which of the points represent large cities or small cities? Legends and other
accompanying text can help, but some ambiguities inevitably remain. The purpose of a visual summary after all
is to present the big picture at the expense of certain detail.

[
5
]
’s approach to this problem was to implement an interactive feature that allows a user to
explore some of these provenance-related questions
in situ
, i.e. directly from the chart. By selecting
an individual point, the user is able to bring up a view showing the data the point was calculated from. In
the figure the highlighted point represents Chiang Mai, and shows that the plotted value of 10.9% was derived
from an increase in population density from 4,416 to 5,010 people per sq. km. Features like these are valuable
as comprehension aids, but are laborious to implement by hand and require the author to anticipate the queries
a user might have. For these reasons they also tend not to generalise: for example
[
5
]
’s
visualisation only allows the user to select one point at a time.

1.1

Data transparency as PL infrastructure

Hand-crafted efforts like
[
5
]
’s are labour-intensive because they involve manually embedding
metadata about the relationship between outputs and inputs into the same program. When the visualisation or
analysis logic changes, the relationship between inputs and outputs also change, and the metadata must be
manually updated. A less costly and more robust approach is to treat data provenance as a language
infrastructure problem, baking lineage or provenance metadata directly into outputs so that provenance queries
can be supported automatically. For example, for an in-memory database engine,
[
31
]

describe how to ‘‘backward trace’’ from output selections to input selections, and then ‘‘forward trace’’ to
find related output selections in other views, to support a popular feature from data visualisation called

linked brushing
.
[
29
]
implemented a similar system for a general-purpose functional
programming language, using execution traces to support bidirectional queries.

The advantage of shifting the burden of implementing transparency features onto the language runtime is that
the author of the content can concentrate purely on data visualisation, and as the infrastructure improves,
the benefits are inherited automatically by end users, at no additional cost to the author. For example if
some kind of formal guarantees are provided (perhaps that data selections are in some sense minimal and
sufficient), then those can be proved once for the infrastructure rather for each bespoke implementation.

In this paper, we propose a new bidirectional analysis framework for a general-purpose programming language,
which supports fine-grained
in situ
provenance queries, an end-user feature we call
data
transparency
. In contrast to prior work, we use
dynamic dependence graphs

[
16
,
2
]

to implement the analyses, which enables our approach to be fast enough for interactive use and also
language-independent, by separating queries over the graph from the problem of deriving the dependence graph
for a particular program.

1.2

Contributions and roadmap

Our specific contributions are as follows:

•

§
3
defines a core calculus for data-transparent outputs, where parts of inputs and
computed values are assigned unique labels called
addresses
, and programs have an operational
semantics that pairs every result with a dynamic dependence graph capturing fine-grained IO
relationships between input addresses and output addresses.

•

§
4
presents a new formal framework for bidirectional provenance queries over
dependence graphs, formalising two operators over such graphs,
\displaystyle\triangledown
(
demands
) and

\displaystyle\triangledown

(
demanded by
). We show these to be
conjugate
in the sense
of
[
21
]
, and give procedures for computing
\displaystyle\triangledown
and

\displaystyle\triangledown

. We show how a
novel cognacy operator
\displaystyle{\triangledown}\!{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}}
called
linked inputs
, relating inputs when they contribute to
common features of the output, can be obtained by composing
\displaystyle\triangledown
and

\displaystyle\triangledown

, and how a
dual
linked outputs
operator
\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}}\!{\triangledown}
, supporting the ‘‘linked visualisations’’ of prior
work
[
31
,
29
]
, is easily obtained by transposing the two operators.

•

§
5
shows that our implementation performs better than one based on traces and
bidirectional interpreters, the primary alternative implementation technique. Using usability metrics
from
[
25
]
, we find that
\displaystyle 81\%
of the queries we tested execute at a speed that appears
instantaneous to a user, compared to
\displaystyle 25\%
using an implementation based on traces. We also compare
overhead of building a trace vs. building a dependence graph for a given program.

These contributions are not available in previous work. In particular, although

[
31
]
support fast queries for linked visualisations in a database setting, and

[
29
]
support linked outputs in a general-purpose languages, ours is (to the best of our knowledge)
the first implementation for a general-purpose language which is fast enough for interactive use. Other
related work in database provenance, program slicing and data visualisation are discussed in
§
6
.

§
7
wraps up with a discussion of some limitations. In particular, the sort of transparency
considered in this paper is purely extensional, and falls short of providing full explanations of how output
parts are related to input parts. We discuss more intensional forms of transparency in §
7
and
propose some other ways in which the present system could be improved.

We implement our approach in a pure functional programming language called [OurLanguage]. The author of a
visualisation expresses their chart as a pure function of the inputs, using a set of built-in data types for
common visualisations; a d3.js front end automatically enriches the rendered outputs with support for
interactive selection and the data transparency queries introduced in the next section. Our implementation is
open source and available online (given later in a deanonymised version). We also provide an anonymised web
demo at
http://opencomputation.org/
.

2

Overview: Fine-Grained Interactive Provenance

In this section, we introduce the main interactive data provenance features that wish to support, using two
[OurLanguage] examples to illustrate. The line chart in Figure
2
shows projected
methane emissions from agricultural sources under a global warming scenario called RCP8.5, with source code in
Figure
3
; the scatter plot and stacked bar chart in
Figure
4
show changing non-renewable energy outputs and capacities for various
countries, with source code in Figure
5
.

The key idea of a transparent visualisation is that the original data sources are kept around and can be
viewed (when a user so requests) alongside the visualisation, and the user is then able to interact with both
the data and the view to explore how they are related. Crucially the author of the visualisation does not to
have to implement any of these interactions themselves; they need only write the visualisation code and the
language runtime and rendering infrastructure provides the interactions.

1. Fine-grained linking of the data to the view.

In Figure
2
, the user has chosen to reveal the underlying data set, which is shown
on the left; only rows relevant to the chart are visible, with the other rows hidden automatically. This
clarifies that only agricultural data is relevant, confirming the (informal) claim in the caption. The
emissions values shown do contribute to the chart in some way, and the user can investigate this by
interacting with individual entries. For example, moving their mouse over the number 104.69 highlights

one
point in the projected emfissions curve, and
three
points in the other curve, which plots the
moving average of the projected emissions. The provenance analysis which underpins this we write as

\displaystyle\triangledown

(‘‘demanded by’’), and here this tells us that 104.69 was needed to compute either the
\displaystyle x
or

\displaystyle y
coordinate (in this case just the
\displaystyle y
coordinate) of the four highlighted points.

(

\displaystyle\triangledown

) and

(
\displaystyle\triangledown
) operators
link inputs that share common output dependencies.

2. Linked inputs.

We want to support fine-grained IO queries that run in the other direction too, via another provenance
analysis written
\displaystyle\triangledown
(‘‘demands’’). In Figure
2
the
\displaystyle\triangledown
analysis
is initiated automatically on the output of

\displaystyle\triangledown

; this reveals that calculating the
\displaystyle y
coordinates
of the points highlighted on the right required not only the 104.69 we started with, but 4 additional
emissions values (blue border on the left). These additional inputs we refer to as the
related inputs

of the original input selection; they are the other inputs demanded by any output that demands our starting
input, and are computed using the composite operator
\displaystyle{\triangledown}\!{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}}
. Given that the initial input selection here
contributes to 3 points of the moving average, the ‘‘window’’ of related inputs in this case is 5 wide,
comprising all the data points needed to account for the selection on the right. Related inputs is a

cognacy
relation: two inputs are related if they have a common ancestor in a
dependence graph

that captures how inputs are demanded by outputs.

3. Fine-grained linking of the view to the data.

We would also like to support cognacy queries that start from the output rather than the input; this is the
basis of a feature called
linked brushing
(or
brushing and linking
) in data visualisation

[
4
,
6
]
. Here we would like to provide linked brushing in a way that is transparent to the
user. In Figure
4
, the user has again revealed the underlying data set, this time
opting to see only rows with active data selections, rather than only rows with data that are used by any part
of the output. They then express interest in one of the points in the scatter plot. By clicking on it, rather
than just moving their mouse over it, they create what we call a
persistent
selection (shown in green).
The demands analysis
\displaystyle\triangledown
reveals (also in green) the inputs needed to compute both the
\displaystyle x
and
\displaystyle y

coordinates of the selected point; this shows that the 2016 data for 4 countries was used, although it does
not reveal how.

4. Linked outputs.

Now that the inputs demanded by the output selection are determined, the

\displaystyle\triangledown

analysis runs
automatically on the output of
\displaystyle\triangledown
; this reveals that those inputs were also needed for 4 of the bar
segments (highlighted with cross-hatching) in the bar chart, namely those for 2016. This is standard linked
brushing, and is implemented using the composite operator
\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}}\!{\triangledown}
, which we call
related outputs
.
Related outputs is a ‘‘co-cognacy’’ relation, relating two outputs whenever they have a common descendant,
rather than common ancestor, in the dependence graph. Here the user can take advantage of the fact that a
persistent selection remains active even when the mouse moves off the original item of interest; this allows
them to investigate further. Moving their mouse over the yellow IND segment of the 2016 bar (highlighted with
blue border) initiates an orthogonal
\displaystyle\triangledown
query which shows (also with various blue borders) the data
needed to compute the yellow segment. Overlaid on the persistent selection, this reveals that the 37.90 in the

nuclearOut
column is needed to compute
both
the IND bar segment
and
the selected scatter
plot point, explaining why the two output selections are linked.

3

A Core Calculus for Transparent Outputs

We start by introducing a calculus for transparent outputs, which is the core of [OurLanguage]. The core language
is fairly expressive, supporting deep pattern-matching and mutual recursion; however [OurLanguage] also provides a
surface syntax that desugars into the core, providing piecewise function definitions, list comprehensions and
other conveniences, as shown in Figures
3
and
5
,
which would otherwise complicate the core.

The term syntax of the core language (§
3.1
) is uncontroversial: it is pure and untyped, and
provides datatypes, records and matrices (although we omit a presentation of matrices here). The syntax of
values is less standard: each part of a value has a unique
address

\displaystyle\alpha
which serves to identify
that part of the value in a dependence graph. We give a big-step operational semantics that evaluates a term
to both a value and a
dynamic dependence graph
for that value (§
3.2
), capturing how
parts of the value depend on parts of the input.

Some notation.

We write to

\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\displaystyle\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil

denote a finite sequence of elements
\displaystyle{x_{1}},\,..,\,{x_{n}}
,
with
\displaystyle\epsilon
as the empty sequence. Concatenation of sequences is written

for snoc (append). We write

\displaystyle\{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\displaystyle\hfil\displaystyle{k}:{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\displaystyle\hfil\textstyle{k}:{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\displaystyle\hfil\scriptstyle{k}:{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\displaystyle\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{k}:{x}\hfil$\crcr}}}\}
to denote a finite map, i.e. a set of pairs

\displaystyle{{k_{1}}:{x_{1}}},\,..,\,{{k_{n}}:{x_{n}}}
where keys
\displaystyle k_{i}
are pairwise unique. If
\displaystyle X
and
\displaystyle Y
are sets,
we write
\displaystyle X\uplus Y
to mean
\displaystyle X\cup Y
where
\displaystyle X
and
\displaystyle Y
are disjoint, and also
\displaystyle x\cdot X
or
\displaystyle X\cdot x
to mean
\displaystyle X\uplus\{x\}
.

Foreign functions

The language is also parameterised by a finite map
\displaystyle\Phi
from variables
\displaystyle f
to arities
\displaystyle\Phi(f)\in\mathbb{N}
of
the foreign function they denote. For the graph semantics in §
3.2
below, every foreign
function name
\displaystyle f
is required to provide an interpretation
\displaystyle\hat{f}
that, for a sequence of arguments

\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\displaystyle\hfil\displaystyle v\hfil

and dependence graph
\displaystyle G
, returns the result of applying the foreign function named by
\displaystyle f
to

\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\displaystyle\hfil\displaystyle v\hfil

plus a dependence graph
\displaystyle G^{\prime}
which extends
\displaystyle G
with information about how the result depends on

\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\displaystyle\hfil\displaystyle v\hfil

.

Continuations and eliminators

A
continuation

\displaystyle\kappa
is a term
\displaystyle e
or an eliminator
\displaystyle\sigma
, describing how an execution proceeds
after a value is matched. An
eliminator
is a deep pattern-matching construct based on
tries
[
20
,
30
]
; for a language with rich structured values like records and data types,
this makes for a cleaner presentation than a single shallow elimination form for each type. Piecewise function
definitions in the surface language desugar into eliminators.

An eliminator specifies how values of a particular shape are matched, and for a given value, determines how
any pattern variables get bound and the continuation
\displaystyle\kappa
which will be executed under those bindings. A
variable eliminator
\displaystyle{x}\mapsto{\kappa}
says how to match any value (as variable
\displaystyle x
) and continue as

\displaystyle\kappa
. A record eliminator
\displaystyle{\{{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\displaystyle\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\displaystyle\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\displaystyle\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\displaystyle\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}}\}}\mapsto{\kappa}
says how to match a record with fields

\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\displaystyle\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil

,
and provides a continuation
\displaystyle\kappa
for sequentially matching the values of those fields. Lastly, a
constructor eliminator
\displaystyle\{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\displaystyle\hfil\displaystyle c\mapsto\kappa\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\displaystyle\hfil\textstyle c\mapsto\kappa\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\displaystyle\hfil\scriptstyle c\mapsto\kappa\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\displaystyle\hfil\scriptscriptstyle c\mapsto\kappa\hfil$\crcr}}}\}
provides a branch
\displaystyle c\mapsto\kappa
for each
constructor
\displaystyle c
in

\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\displaystyle\hfil\displaystyle c\hfil

, where each
\displaystyle\kappa
specifies how any arguments to
\displaystyle c
will be matched. (We
assume that any constructors matched by an eliminator all belong to the same data type, but this is not
enforced in the core language.)

Addresses, values and environments

We define addressed
values

\displaystyle v
mutually inductively with
raw values
. A raw value is simply a
value without an associated address; a value decorates a raw value with an address
\displaystyle\alpha
. Addresses are
allocated during evaluation so that new partial values have fresh addresses, which can then be used as
vertices in a dependence graph.

Raw values
\displaystyle\bm{v}
include integers
\displaystyle n,m
; records

was mutually defined.

3.2

Operational Semantics

We now give a big-step operational semantics for the core language, which evaluates a term to a value paired
with a
dynamic dependence graph
for that value.

Dynamic Dependence Graphs

A
dynamic dependence graph

[
2
]
(hereafter
dependence graph
) is a directed acyclic
graph
\displaystyle G=(V,E)
with a set
\displaystyle V
of
vertices
and a set
\displaystyle E\subseteq V\times V
of edges. When
convenient we write
\displaystyle\textup{{V}}(G)
for
\displaystyle V
and
\displaystyle\textup{{E}}(G)
for
\displaystyle E
. In the dependence graph for a particular program,
vertices
\displaystyle\alpha,\beta\in V
are addresses associated to values (either supplied to the program as inputs or
produced during evaluation) and edges
\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta)\in E
indicate that, in the evaluation of that
program, the value associated to
\displaystyle\beta

depends on
(is
demanded by
, in the terminology of
§
2
) the value associated to
\displaystyle\alpha
. Such values may be sub-terms of a larger value. This
diverges somewhat from traditional approaches to dynamic dependence graphs in only considering one type of
edge (data dependency) rather than separate data and control dependencies; moreover our edges point in the
direction of dependent vertices, whereas in the literature the other direction is somewhat more common.

During evaluation, when a fresh vertex
\displaystyle\alpha
is allocated for a constructed value, the dependence graph
\displaystyle G

is extended by a graph fragment specifying that
\displaystyle\alpha
depends on a set
\displaystyle V
of preexisting vertices, given
by the following notation:

Definition 1
(In-star notation)

Write
\displaystyle\{V\mapsto\alpha\}
as shorthand for the star graph
\displaystyle(\{\alpha\}\uplus V,V\times\{\alpha\})
.

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\displaystyle\hfil\displaystyle v\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\displaystyle\hfil\textstyle v\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\displaystyle\hfil\scriptstyle v\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\displaystyle\hfil\scriptscriptstyle v\hfil$\crcr}}},\kappa\rightsquigarrow\gamma,e,V

\frac{}{\epsilon,e\rightsquigarrow\varnothing,e,\varnothing}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{
lab={{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}{{$\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow$-done}}}}
}\frac{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\displaystyle\hfil\displaystyle v\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\mkern Operational semantics with dependence graph

Pattern matching

Figure
7
defines the pattern-matching judgement
\displaystyle\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern .
Rather than matching a single value
\displaystyle v
, the judgement matches a ‘‘stack’’ of values

\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\displaystyle\hfil\displaystyle v\hfil

against a
continuation
\displaystyle\kappa
, returning the selected branch
\displaystyle e
, an environment
\displaystyle\gamma
providing bindings for the
free variables of
\displaystyle e
, and the set
\displaystyle V
of addresses found in the matched portions of

\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\displaystyle\hfil\displaystyle v\hfil

.

A continuation which is just an expression
\displaystyle e
matches only the empty stack of values
(
\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow
-done
), in which case pattern-matching is complete and
\displaystyle e
is the selected branch. The
other rules require an
eliminator

\displaystyle\sigma
as the continuation and a non-empty stack
\displaystyle v\cdot\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern Evaluation

Figure
8
defines a big-step evaluation relation
\displaystyle\gamma,e,V,G\Rightarrow v,G^{\prime}
stating that term

\displaystyle e
, under an environment
\displaystyle\gamma
, vertex set
\displaystyle V
and dependence graph
\displaystyle G
, evaluates to a value
\displaystyle v
and
extended dependence graph
\displaystyle G^{\prime}
. The vertex set
\displaystyle V
records the (partial) input values consumed by the current
active function call providing the dynamic context in which
\displaystyle e
is being evaluated;
\displaystyle V
is initially empty and
changes whenever a function application is evaluated.

The evaluation rule for variables is fairly standard; the dependence graph is returned unmodified, because no
new addresses are allocated as a result of simply looking up a variable. The rule for record projections

\displaystyle e.y
is similar: if
\displaystyle e
evalutes to a record of the form
\displaystyle\{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\displaystyle\hfil\displaystyle{x}:{v}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\displaystyle\hfil\textstyle{x}:{v}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\displaystyle\hfil\scriptstyle{x}:{v}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\displaystyle\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{x}:{v}\hfil$\crcr}}}\cdot({y}:{u})\}_{\alpha}
, then
\displaystyle e.y
evalutes to the value
\displaystyle u
of field
\displaystyle y
, discarding the
address
\displaystyle\alpha
of the record.

Introduction rules, such as for integers, functions, records and constructors, follow the pattern of
assigning a fresh address for the (partial) value being constructed, and then extending
\displaystyle G
with a set of
dependency edges from the vertices in
\displaystyle V
to
\displaystyle\alpha
. For example, the integer rule evaluates an expression

\displaystyle n
to its value form
\displaystyle n_{\alpha}
; the
\displaystyle\alpha\notin\textup{{V}}(G)
constraint ensures that
\displaystyle\alpha
is fresh. The
dependence graph is then extended with
\displaystyle\{V\mapsto\alpha\}
, indicating that
\displaystyle n_{\alpha}
depended on all
matched partial inputs in
\displaystyle V
. Likewise, the rule for an anonymous function
\displaystyle\lambda{\sigma}
constructs the
closure
\displaystyle{\text{{cl}}}({\gamma},{\varnothing},{\sigma})_{\alpha}
with fresh address
\displaystyle\alpha
, capturing the current
environment
\displaystyle\gamma
and using
\displaystyle\varnothing
as the set of mutually recursive definitions associated with the
function, and again establishing dependency edges from
\displaystyle V
to
\displaystyle\alpha_{i}
.

of the subterms. For example the auxiliary evaluation relation

The rules for
\displaystyle{\text{{let}}}\;{x}\mathrel{{\text{{=}}}}{e}\;{\text{{in}}}\;{e^{\prime}}
and application
\displaystyle{e}\,{e^{\prime}}
may involve mutual recursion, and rely on the
auxiliary relation
\displaystyle\gamma,\rho,V,G\rightarrowtail\gamma^{\prime},G^{\prime}
defined at the bottom of Figure
8
.
This judgement takes a set
\displaystyle\rho
of recursive definitions, vertex set
\displaystyle V
and dependence graph
\displaystyle G
, and
returns an environment
\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}
of closures derived from
\displaystyle\rho
and extended dependence graph
\displaystyle G
. Each
function definition
\displaystyle\rho(x_{i})\in\rho
generates a new closure
\displaystyle(\gamma,\rho,\rho(x_{i}))_{\alpha_{i}}

capturing
\displaystyle\gamma
and
\displaystyle\rho
, with fresh address
\displaystyle{\alpha_{i}}
, and extends the dependence graph with a set of
edges from
\displaystyle V
to
\displaystyle\alpha
.

The evaluation rule for
\displaystyle{\text{{let}}}\;{\rho}\;{\text{{in}}}\;{e}
is similar to the rule for regular let-bindings, except for using

\displaystyle\rightarrowtail
to build a set of closures
\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}
which is used to extend
\displaystyle\gamma
. Finally, the rule for

\displaystyle{e}\,{e^{\prime}}
is notable because this is where the active function context changes and the ambient
\displaystyle V
is
discarded. We compute the closure
\displaystyle{\text{{cl}}}({\gamma_{1}},{\rho},{\sigma})_{\alpha}
from
\displaystyle e
and the argument
\displaystyle v^{\prime}

from
\displaystyle e^{\prime}
, and then use
\displaystyle\sigma
to match
\displaystyle v^{\prime}
. If pattern-matching returns selected branch
\displaystyle e^{\prime\mkern-2.6mu\prime\mkern-2.2mu}
,
with vertex set
\displaystyle V^{\prime}
representing the consumed part of
\displaystyle v^{\prime}
, then
\displaystyle e^{\prime\mkern-2.6mu\prime\mkern-2.2mu}
is evaluated and becomes the
result of the application, with
\displaystyle V^{\prime}\cup\{\alpha\}
serving as set of (partial) inputs associated with the
new active function context.

Example

Figure
9
shows part of the dependence graph for the moving average example in
Figures
2
and
3
. The graph shows the calculation of the
first 3 points in the moving average plot from entries in the
emissions
table. This is a simplified
version of the graph, since in practise they get very large; for example we omit closures, list cells and the
calculation of the divisors. Also note that the node labels here are merely illustrative: the graph only
stores value dependencies, and in particular the in-neighbours of a vertex are unordered.

4

Cognacy Queries Over Dependence Graphs

We now turn to cognacy and ‘‘co-cognacy’’ queries over dependence graphs, which are expressed in terms of the

\displaystyle\triangledown
and

\displaystyle\triangledown

operators introduced informally in §
2
. Boolean algebras
(Definition
2
) are used to represent selections; we then define
\displaystyle\triangledown
and

\displaystyle\triangledown

and their De Morgan duals, first for an arbitrary relation (§
4.1
)
and then for the
IO relation
of a dependence graph (§
4.2
).
Then we give procedures for computing
\displaystyle\triangledown
and

\displaystyle\triangledown

and their duals over a given dependence
graph, via an intermediate graph slice (§
4.3
).

Definition 2
(Boolean algebra)

A
Boolean algebra
(or
Boolean lattice
)
\displaystyle A
is a 6-tuple
\displaystyle(A,\wedge,\vee,\bot,\top,\neg)

with carrier
\displaystyle A
, distinguished elements
\displaystyle\bot
(bottom) and
\displaystyle\top
(top), commutative operations
\displaystyle\wedge

(meet) and
\displaystyle\vee
(join) with
\displaystyle\top
and
\displaystyle\bot
as respective units, and unary operation
\displaystyle\neg
(negate)
satisfying
\displaystyle x\vee\neg x=\top
and
\displaystyle x\wedge\neg x=\bot
. The operations
\displaystyle\wedge
and
\displaystyle\vee
distribute
over each other and induce a partial order
\displaystyle\leq
over
\displaystyle X
.

An input (or output) selection, where
\displaystyle X
is the set of all addresses that occur in the input (or output) of
the program, is represented by the
powerset
Boolean algebra
\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X)
whose carrier is the set of
subsets
\displaystyle X^{\prime}\subseteq X
ordered by inclusion
\displaystyle\subseteq
. In this case bottom is the empty set
\displaystyle\varnothing
,
top is the whole set
\displaystyle X
, meet and join are given by
\displaystyle\cap
and
\displaystyle\cup
, and negation by relative complement

\displaystyle\setminus
, so that
\displaystyle\neg X^{\prime}=X\setminus X^{\prime}
.

4.1

\displaystyle\triangledown
and

\displaystyle\triangledown

and their duals

The
\displaystyle\triangledown
and

\displaystyle\triangledown

operators sketched earlier are simply the image and preimage functions for
a particular relation
\displaystyle R
.

Definition 3
(Image and Preimage Functions for a Relation)

For a relation
\displaystyle R\subseteq X\times Y
, define
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{R}:\mathbb{P}(X)\to\mathbb{P}(Y)
and

\displaystyle\triangledown_{R}:\mathbb{P}(Y)\to\mathbb{P}(X)
as:

1.

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{R}(X^{\prime}):=\{y\in Y\mid\exists x\in X^{\prime}.(x,y)\in R\}
(image)

2.

\displaystyle\triangledown_{R}(Y^{\prime}):=\{x\in X\mid\exists y\in Y^{\prime}.(x,y)\in R\}
(preimage)

Trivially
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{R}=\triangledown_{R^{-1}}
. The image and preimage functions form a

conjugate pair
, in the sense of
[
21
]
:

Definition 4
(Conjugate Functions)

For Boolean algebras
\displaystyle A
,
\displaystyle B
, functions
\displaystyle f:A\to B
and
\displaystyle g:B\to A
form a
conjugate pair
iff:

f(x)\wedge y=\bot\iff x\wedge g(y)=\bot

Lemma 1

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{R}
and
\displaystyle\triangledown_{R}
are conjugate.

This should be intuitive enough: for any subsets
\displaystyle X^{\prime}\subseteq X
and
\displaystyle Y^{\prime}\subseteq Y
, if the elements ‘‘on
the right’’ to which
\displaystyle X^{\prime}
is related are disjoint from
\displaystyle Y^{\prime}
, then there are no edges in
\displaystyle R
from
\displaystyle X^{\prime}
to
\displaystyle Y^{\prime}
;
and in virtue of that, the elements ‘‘on the left’’ to which
\displaystyle Y^{\prime}
is related must also be disjoint from
\displaystyle X^{\prime}
.

Conjugate functions are related to another class of near-reciprocals between Boolean algebras, namely

Galois connections
; in fact every conjugate pair induces a Galois connection
[
23
]
. This
relates the present setting to previous work on program slicing with Galois
connections
[
28
,
27
,
32
]
.

Definition 5
(Galois connection)

Suppose
\displaystyle A,B
are partial orders. Then monotone functions
\displaystyle f:A\to B
and
\displaystyle g:B\to A
form a
Galois
connection
iff

f(x)\leq y\iff x\leq g(y)

Proposition 1

Suppose functions
\displaystyle f:A\to B
and
\displaystyle g:B\to A
between Boolean algebras. The following statements are
equivalent:

1.

\displaystyle f
and
\displaystyle g
form a conjugate pair

2.

\displaystyle f
and
\displaystyle g^{\circ}
form a Galois connection

It is also useful (both for performance reasons and as a user feature) to consider the
De Morgan duals

of
\displaystyle\triangledown
and

\displaystyle\triangledown

, which we write as
\displaystyle\blacktriangle
and
\displaystyle\blacktriangledown
; these also form a
conjugate pair.

Definition 6
(De Morgan Dual)

Suppose a function
\displaystyle f:A\to B
between Boolean algebras with
\displaystyle\neg_{A}
and
\displaystyle\neg_{B}
the negation operators
of
\displaystyle A
and
\displaystyle B
. Define the
De Morgan Dual

\displaystyle f^{\circ}:A\to B
of
\displaystyle f
as:

f^{\circ}:=\neg_{B}\circ f\circ\neg_{A}

Definition 7
(Dual Image and Preimage Functions for a Relation)

For relations
\displaystyle R\subseteq X\times Y
, define
\displaystyle\blacktriangle_{R}:\mathbb{P}(X)\to\mathbb{P}(Y)
and

\displaystyle\blacktriangledown_{R}:\mathbb{P}(Y)\to\mathbb{P}(X)
as:

1.

\displaystyle\blacktriangle_{R}(X^{\prime}):=\{y\in Y\mid\nexists x\in X\setminus X^{\prime}.(x,y)\in R\}
(dual image)

2.

\displaystyle\blacktriangledown_{R}(Y^{\prime}):=\{x\in X\mid\nexists y\in Y\setminus Y^{\prime}.(x,y)\in R\}
(dual preimage)

Bearing in mind that
\displaystyle\neg
for
\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X)
is just relative complement
\displaystyle X\setminus\cdot
, it is easy to
show that these are indeed the intended De Morgan duals.

Lemma 2
(Duality of image and preimage functions)

1.

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{R}^{\circ}=\blacktriangle_{R}

2.

\displaystyle\triangledown_{R}^{\circ}=\blacktriangledown_{R}

If
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{R}(X^{\prime})
picks out the outputs that the elements of
\displaystyle X^{\prime}
are
necessary
for,

\displaystyle\blacktriangle_{R}(X^{\prime})
picks out the outputs that the elements of
\displaystyle X
are
sufficient
for.

\displaystyle\blacktriangledown_{R}(Y^{\prime})
picks out the inputs that are needed
only
by elements of
\displaystyle Y^{\prime}
.

4.2

\displaystyle\triangledown_{G}
and
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}
for Dependence Graphs

Reachability in
\displaystyle G
induces a relation just between
sources
and
sinks
, which we call the

IO
relation of
\displaystyle G
. We now extend
\displaystyle\triangledown_{R}
and
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{R}
and their duals to a
dependence graph
\displaystyle G
, via its IO relation.

Definition 8
(Sources and sinks)

For a graph
\displaystyle G=(V,E)
, write
\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(G)
for the
sources
of
\displaystyle G
(i.e. those vertices with no
in-edges) and
\displaystyle\mathsf{T}(G)
for the
sinks
of
\displaystyle G
(those with no out-edges).

Definition 9
(Reachability relation)

Define the
reachability relation
for a graph
\displaystyle G=(V,E)
to be the reflexive transitive closure of
\displaystyle E
.

Definition 10
(IO relation)

For any dependence graph
\displaystyle G
with reachability relation
\displaystyle R
, define the
IO
relation of
\displaystyle G
to be
\displaystyle R\cap(\mathsf{S}(G)\times\mathsf{T}(G))
.

The IO relation specifies how specific inputs (sources) are demanded by specific outputs (sinks); thus we
interpret
\displaystyle(x,y)\in R
as ‘‘
\displaystyle x
is
demanded by

\displaystyle y
’’. Clearly the IO relation of
\displaystyle G
is the
converse of the IO relation of
\displaystyle G^{-1}
. The set of inputs that some outputs
\displaystyle Y

demand
,

\displaystyle\triangledown_{G}(Y)
, is simply the subset of the inputs of
\displaystyle G
reachable from
\displaystyle Y
(by traversing the graph in
its opposite direction). Conversely, the set of outputs
demanded by
some inputs
\displaystyle X
,

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}(X)
, is simply the subset of the outputs of
\displaystyle G
reachable from
\displaystyle X
.

Definition 11
(
\displaystyle\triangledown_{G}
and
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}
for a dependence graph)

For a graph
\displaystyle G
with IO relation
\displaystyle R
, define:

1.

\displaystyle\triangledown_{G}:=\triangledown_{R}:\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{T}(G))\to\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{S}(G))
(demands)

2.

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}:=\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{R}:\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{S}(G))\to\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{T}(G))
(demanded by)

4.3

Computing
\displaystyle\triangledown_{G}
,
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}
,
\displaystyle\blacktriangle_{G}
and
\displaystyle\blacktriangledown_{G}
for
Dependence Graphs

We now show how to compute
\displaystyle\triangledown_{G}
,
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}
,
\displaystyle\blacktriangle_{G}
and
\displaystyle\blacktriangledown_{G}
for
a dependence graph
\displaystyle G
, via an intermediate graph slice which we then restrict to its IO relation. First some
graph notation:

Definition 12
(In-edges and out-edges)

For a graph
\displaystyle G=(V,E)
and vertex
\displaystyle\alpha\in V
, write
\displaystyle\textup{{inE}}_{G}(\alpha)
for the in-edges of
\displaystyle\alpha
in
\displaystyle G

and
\displaystyle\textup{{outE}}_{G}(\alpha)
for its out-edges.

Definition 13
(Opposite graph)

For a graph
\displaystyle G
define the
opposite
graph
\displaystyle G^{-1}:=(V,E^{-1})
where
\displaystyle\cdot^{-1}
denotes
relational converse.

With the trace-based approaches mentioned in §
1
, one can use a given algorithm to implement
its De Morgan dual; for example given a procedure for
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}
we can compute
\displaystyle\blacktriangle_{G}
by
pre- and post-composing with negation
[
29
]
. In the dependence graph setting we can also use a given
algorithm to compute its conjugate: for example given a procedure for
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}
, we can compute

\displaystyle\triangledown_{G}
simply as
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G^{-1}}
.

For efficiency, however, we give direct procedures both for
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}

(§
4.3
) and
\displaystyle\blacktriangle_{G}
(§
4.3
), which
can then be used to derive implementations of the other operators (Figure
12

below). Each procedure factors through an auxiliary operation that computes a ‘‘slice’’ of the original graph,
i.e. a contiguous subgraph. While it is technically possible to compute the desired image/preimage of the IO
relation without creating this intermediate graph, we anticipate use cases which will make use of the graph
slice; these are discussed in more detail in §
7
. Our implementation makes it easy to flip
between
\displaystyle G
and
\displaystyle G^{-1}
so we freely make use of both in the algorithms to access out-neighbours and
in-neighbours.

Direct algorithm for
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}
(
demanded by
)

Definition 14
(
\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{demBy}}_{G}}
)

Figure
10(a)
defines the family of relations
\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{demBy}}_{G}}
for dependence graph
\displaystyle G
.

For a dependence graph
\displaystyle G
and inputs
\displaystyle X\subseteq\mathsf{S}(G)
, the judgement
\displaystyle X\;\smash{\mathrel{\textup{{demBy}}_{G}}}Y
says that
\displaystyle X
is demanded by
\displaystyle Y\subseteq\mathsf{T}(G)
. The algorithm defers to an auxiliary operation

\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{demByV}}}
, which takes a (partial) graph slice
\displaystyle H\subseteq G
, initially containing only the original
input vertices
\displaystyle X
and no edges, and which proceeds as follows. If there is a sink of
\displaystyle H
that still has
outgoing edges in
\displaystyle G
, then the targets of those edges are reachable from the vertices of
\displaystyle H
, and so we must
add them to
\displaystyle H
and recurse (
extend
). If
\displaystyle\mathsf{T}(H)\subseteq\mathsf{T}(G)
, then there are no
unexplored nodes in
\displaystyle G
that are reachable from
\displaystyle H
, and so we terminate with the current state of
\displaystyle H

(
done
). We note that it is enough to consider the sinks of
\displaystyle H
, since we move every outgoing edge
of a vertex from
\displaystyle G
to
\displaystyle H
all at once. When
\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{demByV}}}
is done,
\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{demBy}}_{G}}
returns the sinks

\displaystyle T(H)
from the final graph slice
\displaystyle H
, representing the outputs that the original inputs are demanded by.

Proposition 2
(
\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{demBy}}_{G}}
Computes
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}
)

For any dependence graph
\displaystyle G
, any
\displaystyle X\subseteq\mathsf{S}(G)
and any
\displaystyle Y\subseteq\mathsf{T}(G)
we have

X\mathrel{\textup{{demBy}}_{G}}Y\iff\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}(X)=Y

Proof

See §
0.A.4
.

\displaystyle X\mathrel{\textup{{demBy}}_{G}}Y

\frac{(X,\varnothing),G\mathrel{\textup{{demByV}}}H}{X\mathrel{\textup{{demBy}}_{G}}\mathsf{T}(H)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{
right={$\displaystyle X\subseteq\mathsf{S}(G)$}
}

\displaystyle H,G\mathrel{\textup{{demByV}}}H^{\prime}

\frac{}{H,G\mathrel{\textup{{demByV}}}H}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{
lab={{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}{{done}}}},
right={$\displaystyle\mathsf{T}(H)\subseteq\mathsf{T}(G)$}
}\frac{H\cup E,G\setminus_{\mathsf{E}}E\mathrel{\textup{{demByV}}}H^{\prime}}{H,G\mathrel{\textup{{demByV}}}H^{\prime}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{
lab={{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}{{extend}}}},
right={$\displaystyle\alpha\in\mathsf{T}(H)\wedge E=\textup{{outE}}_{G}(\alpha)\neq\varnothing$}
}

(a)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{demBy}}_{G}}
algorithm

\displaystyle X\mathrel{\textup{{suff}}_{G}}U

\frac{(X,\varnothing),(\varnothing,\varnothing),G\mathrel{\textup{{suffE}}}H}{X\mathrel{\textup{{suff}}_{G}}\textup{{V}}(H)\cap\mathsf{T}(G)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{
right=$\displaystyle X\subseteq\mathsf{S}(G)$
}

\displaystyle H,P,G\mathrel{\textup{{suffE}}}H^{\prime}

\frac{}{H,P,G\mathrel{\textup{{suffE}}}H}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{
lab={{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}{{done}}}},
right={$\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(G)\cap\textup{{V}}(P)\subseteq\mathsf{S}(P)\wedge\textup{{V}}(H)\subseteq\mathsf{T}(G)$}
}\frac{H,P\cup E,G\setminus_{\mathsf{E}}E\mathrel{\textup{{suffE}}}H^{\prime}}{H,P,G\mathrel{\textup{{suffE}}}H^{\prime}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{
lab={{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}{{pending}}}},
right={$\displaystyle\alpha\in\textup{{V}}(H)\wedge E=\textup{{outE}}_{G}(\alpha)\neq\varnothing$}
}\frac{H\cup E,P\setminus_{\mathsf{E}}E,G\mathrel{\textup{{suffE}}}H^{\prime}}{H,P,G\mathrel{\textup{{suffE}}}H^{\prime}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{
lab={{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}{{extend}}}},
right={$\displaystyle\alpha\in\mathsf{S}(G)\wedge E=\textup{{inE}}_{P}(\alpha)\neq\varnothing$}
}

(b)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{suff}}_{G}}
algorithm

Figure 10
:
Dual analyses over a dependence graph
\displaystyle G

Direct algorithm for
\displaystyle\blacktriangle_{G}
(
suffices
)

Definition 15
(
\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{suff}}_{G}}
)

Figure
10(b)
defines the family of relations
\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{suff}}_{G}}
for dependence graph
\displaystyle G
.

Like
\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{demands}}_{G}}
, the algorithm
\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{suff}}_{G}}
also delegates to an auxiliary operation
\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{suffE}}}

which builds a slice of
\displaystyle G
. The operation
\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{suffE}}}
takes the graph slice
\displaystyle H
under construction, a
‘‘pending’’ subgraph
\displaystyle P
of nodes for which we have discovered partial information, and the remaining
unexplored graph
\displaystyle G
, and returns the final graph slice
\displaystyle H^{\prime}
. Whenever we have a vertex in
\displaystyle H
which still has
outgoing edges in
\displaystyle G
, we add those edges and their endpoints to the pending graph
\displaystyle P
(
pending
).
If a vertex
\displaystyle\alpha
has no more incoming edges in
\displaystyle G
, we can then move
\displaystyle\alpha
and its incoming edges from

\displaystyle P
to
\displaystyle H
(
extend
), as this only happens when we have already moved every ancestor of
\displaystyle\alpha

into
\displaystyle H
. If neither scenario is the case, then we terminate with the (potentially incomplete) graph

\displaystyle H
(
done
). Once
\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{suffE}}}
has terminated with
\displaystyle H
,
\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{suff}}_{G}}
returns just the
vertices in
\displaystyle H
that are also sinks in
\displaystyle G
. If there are no such vertices, it simply means that the input data

\displaystyle X
was insufficient to compute any of the original program’s outputs.

We provide an example of a run of the algorithm in Figure
11
. Here,
the green vertices and thick edges are in
\displaystyle H
, the orange vertices and dashed arrows are in
\displaystyle P
, and the
blue vertices and normal arrows are in
\displaystyle G
. In the figure, steps 2, 3, 5 and 6 correspond to applications
of the rule
pending
, and steps 4 and 7 correspond to applications of the rule
extend
.
After step 7, the run is complete.

:
Run of
\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{suff}}}
with
\displaystyle G
and
\displaystyle H
superimposed on
\displaystyle G_{0}

Proposition 3
(
\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{suff}}_{G}}
Computes
\displaystyle\blacktriangle_{G}
)

For any dependence graph
\displaystyle G
, any
\displaystyle X\subseteq\mathsf{S}(G)
and any
\displaystyle Y\subseteq\mathsf{T}(G)
we have:

X\mathrel{\textup{{suff}}_{G}}Y\iff\blacktriangle_{g}(X)=Y

Proof

See §
0.A.6
.

Derived algorithms

Propositions
2
and
3
justify treating

\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{demBy}}_{G}}
and
\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{suff}}_{G}}
as functions. Using Lemma
2
we can
define a direct algorithm for
\displaystyle\triangledown_{G}
from
\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{demBy}}_{G}}
by simply flipping the graph, and the
same can be done to acquire an algorithm for
\displaystyle\blacktriangledown_{G}
from
\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{suff}}_{G}}
. Using
Lemma
2
and Definition
6
we can also define alternative
algorithms for all 4 operators using the De Morgan dual construction. All algorithms are summarised in
Figure
12
.

Abstract operator

Alternative algorithm

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{demBy}}_{G}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{suff}}_{G}}^{\circ}

\displaystyle\triangledown_{G}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{demBy}}_{G^{-1}}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{suff}}_{G^{-1}}}^{\circ}

\displaystyle\blacktriangle_{G}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{suff}}_{G}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{demBy}}_{G}}^{\circ}

\displaystyle\blacktriangledown_{G}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{demBy}}_{G^{-1}}}^{\circ}

:
Direct and alternative (De Morgan Dual) algorithms for all 4 operators

In §
5
we contrast the performance of the direct and De Morgan dual implementations
of
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}
, which is an essential component of the linked inputs and linked outputs operators
introduced in §
1
.

4.4

\displaystyle{\triangledown}\!{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}}_{G}
and
\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}}\!{\triangledown}_{G}
for Dependence Graphs

Now we can provide a formal account of the notions of linked inputs and linked outputs.

Definition 16
(Linked inputs and outputs)

For a dependence graph
\displaystyle G
define:

1.

\displaystyle{\triangledown}\!{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}}_{G}:=\triangledown_{G}\circ\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}
(linked inputs)

2.

\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}}\!{\triangledown}_{G}:=\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}\circ\triangledown_{G}
(linked outputs)

Intuitively, linked outputs and linked inputs are relations of
cognacy
(common ancestry) in
\displaystyle G
and

\displaystyle G^{-1}
respectively. For a set of inputs
\displaystyle X\subseteq\mathsf{S}(G)
, linked inputs asks ‘‘what other
inputs are demanded by the outputs which demand X?’’; it first finds all outputs
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}(X)
that
our inputs are demanded by, and then computes the inputs that those outputs demand,
i.e.
\displaystyle\triangledown_{G}(\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}(X^{\prime}))
. Conversely for a set of outputs
\displaystyle Y\subseteq\mathsf{T}(G)
, linked outputs asks
‘‘what other outputs demand the inputs that Y demands’’; it first finds all inputs
\displaystyle\triangledown_{G}(Y^{\prime})
that
our outputs demand, and then computes the outputs that those inputs
\displaystyle\triangledown_{G}(Y)
demand,
i.e.
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}(\triangledown_{G}(Y))
.

In general, neither
\displaystyle{\triangledown}\!{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}}_{G}
nor
\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}}\!{\triangledown}_{G}
is inflationary; unputs which are not used anywhere
are not preserved by the
\displaystyle{\triangledown}\!{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}}_{G}
round-trip, and outputs which don’t require any inputs are not
preserved by the
\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}}\!{\triangledown}_{G}
round-trip. Thus in general we have neither
\displaystyle X^{\prime}\subseteq{\triangledown}\!{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}}_{G}(X^{\prime})
nor
\displaystyle X^{\prime}\subseteq{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}}\!{\triangledown}_{G}(X^{\prime})
. However if we constrain things so that there are
no inputs which are unused, then
\displaystyle\triangledown_{G}
will preserve
\displaystyle\bot
, i.e.
\displaystyle\triangledown_{G}(\varnothing)=\varnothing
, and
\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}}\!{\triangledown}_{G}
will preserve the original output selection. Similarly, if we constrain
things so that all outputs demand some input, then
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}
will preserve
\displaystyle\bot
, and
\displaystyle{\triangledown}\!{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}}_{G}

will preserve the original input selection:

Lemma 3

1.

If
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}(\varnothing)=\varnothing
, then for all
\displaystyle X
we have
\displaystyle{\triangledown}\!{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}}_{G}(X)\geq X
.

2.

If
\displaystyle\triangledown_{G}(\varnothing)=\varnothing
, then for all
\displaystyle Y
we have
\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}}\!{\triangledown}_{G}(Y)\geq Y
.

As an easy corollary, if
\displaystyle\triangledown_{G}
and
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}
both preserve
\displaystyle\varnothing
then in fact they form
an antitone Galois connection. In practical terms, it is possible to use this to produce a filtered view, only
showing inputs that are actually used in a program.

5

Evaluation

We now compare our dependence graph approach with the main alternative style of implementation, namely a
bidirectional interpreter with two components: a forward evaluator which performs a forwards analysis and
produces an execution trace, and a backward evaluator which consumes the execution trace and performs a
backwards analysis
[
32
,
31
,
29
]
.

For a language implementor, the main benefit of our graph approach is that it removes the need to implement a
bidirectional interpreter. Otherwise, changing the language requires modifying each direction of the
interpreter in a manner that maintains bidirectionality --- a considerable effort. In our approach the graph
algorithms for computing
\displaystyle\triangledown_{G}
and
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}
(Figure
10
) are
language-agnostic and only have to be defined once.

For a user, our hypothesis is that our approach has better performance than trace-based techniques. To test
this, we compare two implementations of the dependency-tracking runtime of [OurLanguage], one based on the
dependence graph design described in Sections
3
and
4
, and one based on execution traces and a
bidirectional interpreter. The other system components were shared by the two implementations, including the
parser, desugaring layer, visualisation layer and libraries. We wrote various benchmark programs taking
examples from data analysis and data visualisation (§
5
below).

In an interactive system, performance plays a critical role in a user’s experience. We therefore measured the
following:

•

Q1: Overhead of building a dependence graph versus building a trace;

•

Q2: Performance of computing
demands
over a graph vs. a trace;

•

Q3: Performance of various implementations of
demanded by
: trace-based, graph-based using

\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{demands}}}
, and graph-based using the dual of
\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{suff}}}
.

We assessed performance according to the guidelines proposed by
[
25
]
:

•

100ms: limit for an interaction feeling
instantaneous
;

•

1000ms: limit for a user’s train of thought to remain uninterrupted, although
they may perceive a delay;

•

10000ms: limit to keep a user’s attention on the system.

Ideally, interactive queries would stay as close as possible to the
instantaneous
category
(Q2 and Q3), and the time taken to evaluate programs (Q1) would stay within the limit for keeping a user’s
attention. Expriments were timed on an Intel Core i7-10850H 2.70GHz with 16Gb of RAM, using Google Chrome
121.0.6261.69 and JavaScript runtime V8 12.2.281.16. We used PureScript as the host language.

Choice of examples

We tested our system on a range of data analysis and visualisation benchmarks implemented in [OurLanguage]. First,
we considered matrix convolution. Given that different kernels give rise to subtly different dependency
structures, we implemented three different kernels (
edge-detect
,
emboss
,
gaussian
,
31 lines of code (LOC) each). Second, we developed a full-featured graphics library based on SVG that
demonstrates the performance on bespoke visualisation code (
grouped-bar-chart
, 140 LOC,

line-chart
, 143 LOC,
stacked-bar-chart
, 136 LOC). Finally, we tested two examples that use a
D3.js front end for visualisation (
stacked-bar-scatter-plot
, 26 LOC, and

bar-chart-line-chart
, 38 LOC), the same front end used in
Figures
2
and
4
. The code for our benchmarks is given
in §
0.B
.

In the experiments, each benchmark is run 10 times, and we report the mean runtime and standard deviation (in
parentheses, coloured grey). Runtimes are reported in milliseconds, to 1 decimal place. When we refer to

speedup
or
slowdown
in reference to a pair of implementations, we mean the ratio of average
runtimes for that benchmark.

Q1: Overhead of graph construction vs. trace construction

Table
1
compares the average time taken to evaluate a program in the graph approach
(
G-Eval
) with the time taken in the trace-based approach (
T-Eval
). The ratio of graph-based
to trace-based time is
shown in the third column (
Eval-Slowdown
). Since building the graph involves maintaining a heap of
allocated vertices, and we also build the inverse graph at the same time, we expect a larger overhead for the
graph approach. As expected, we do see a higher overhead, being from 2.5x to 15x slower than their
trace-based counterparts. For both approaches, program evaluation always stays within the limit to keep a
user’s attention on the system (10000ms).

Q2: Graph-based demands vs. trace-based demands

In Table
2
, columns
T-Demands
and
G-Demands
show the average times taken
to compute
demands
with the trace and graph approaches, respectively. The ratio of graph-based to
trace-based performance is in the last column,
Bwd-Speedup
. We compute
demands
over
the graph by running the algorithm for
demBy
from Figure
10(a)
over the
opposite graph. In these examples, we keep our query selections small, generally only selecting one or two
output nodes.

Overall, we observe a significant speedup in the performance of
demands
using the graph approach,
from 33x speedup for the
stacked-bar-chart
, to more than 80x for
gaussian
. In particular,
every graph-based
demands
query is within the ‘‘instantaneous’’ category, whilst all but two of the
examples using the trace approach are in the ‘‘noticable delay’’ category. We attribute this speedup to a
couple of factors. First, in the trace approach, each backwards query involves rewinding the entire execution,
regardless of whether all of the trace is relevant; graph queries only traverse the relevant parts of the
graph. Second, in the trace approach, backwards evaluation makes frequent use of lattice join (
\displaystyle\vee
) to
combine slicing information from different branches of the computation; joining closure slices in particular
has the potential to be expensive because closures contain environments (which in turn contain closures, and
so on). The graph approach lends itself to a more imperative implementation style, where demand information
accrues against vertices as the backwards analysis proceeds, with no separate join steps.
