Discussion of the results

When comparing the performance of the graph and trace approaches, we observe that the only consistent
performance loss for the graph approach is due to the increased cost of evaluating a program to a graph versus
a trace (
Q1
). For our applications, we argue that this overhead is worth paying; the graph need only
be constructed once, and moreover evaluation time remains within the limit for keeping the user’s attention on
the system. More importantly, the fast response times for
demands
and
demanded by
compared
to the trace approach (Q2 and Q3) are good enough for instantaneous queries.

Also notable (observed for Q3), is the performance of
G-DemBy-Suff
, which uses the dual of the

\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{suff}}}
algorithm. In the majority of cases, this performs significantly worse than
G-DemBy
.
The relative difference in performance can perhaps be explained by the fact that our initial selections are
small: since the selection is complemented, and the portion of the graph that the algorithm traverses is in
geneneral much larger. It is also plausible that some of this disparity can be explained by
\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{suff}}}

requiring more book-keeping than
\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{demBy}}}
.

6

Related work

6.1

Data Provenance

Data provenance research has a long history, ranging from document-level workflow provenance

[
8
,
11
]
to more fine-grained database techniques like
where
provenance

[
7
]
, with comparatively less emphasis on general-purpose languages.
[
13
]
track both
value and control dependencies in recursive database queries and user-defined functions, implementing their
approach by rewriting SQL queries to provenance-tracking counterparts;
[
15
]
explore provenance
for language-integrated query, extending the multi-tier language Links
[
10
]
with provenance
tracking, but only for the embedded SQL part of the language. In terms of fine-grained techniques for
structured outputs like visualisations, the most closely related lines of work are
[
31
]

for relational languages, and
[
29
]
for general-purpose languages. These authors all emphasise the
importance of
cognacy
, i.e. common ancestry, albeit not in the context of dependence graphs, showing
how to ‘‘link’’ outputs by tracing back from an output selection to a data selection and then forward to
another output selection. These relations of common ancestry seem to be largely unstudied in the data
provenance literature, despite their importance in data visualisation.

6.2

Dynamic Dependence Graphs

Dynamic dependence graphs have been used extensively for program slicing
[
17
,
18
]
, optimisation

[
16
]
, incremental computation
[
1
]
and fault localisation
[
36
]
. Most of
these applications are for imperative languages, where it is useful distinguish between control and data
edges; because [OurLanguage] is pure, we use somewhat simpler dependence graphs with a single kind of edge. However
different kinds of edge are likely to be needed for future applications (§
7.1
). The main
contribution of our work to the dependence graph paradigm are cognacy operators which allow the identification
of minimally related sets of inputs (
\displaystyle{\triangledown}\!{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}}
) and minimally related sets of outputs (
\displaystyle{\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}}\!{\triangledown}
), in a
formal setting where we show these operators to be self-conjugate and related to Galois connections.

6.3

Galois Slicing

Although we do not consider program slices in the present work, our formal setting is closely related to
program slicing techniques based on Galois connections
[
28
,
27
,
32
]
. In these works,
which use a bidirectional interpreter, the forward and backward directions of the interpreter correspond to
our
sufficiency
(
\displaystyle\blacktriangle
) and
demands
(
\displaystyle\triangledown
) queries.
[
29
]
extended this
approach to a setting where slices have complements, and showed how composing the backward analysis with the
De Morgan dual of the forwards analysis can be used to implement linked brushing. As well as requiring a
bidirectional interpreter, the main difficulty with this approach has been achieving interactive performance.
Implementations have relied on a sequential execution trace, used in the forwards direction to ‘‘replay’’ the
computation, propagating sufficiency information from inputs to outputs, and in the backwards direction to
‘‘rewind’’ the computation, propagating demand from outputs to inputs. As discussed in §
5
,
and in contrast to the graph approach presented here, this approach is overly sequential and unable to exploit
the independence of some subcomputations that makes slicing useful in the first place.

6.4

Linked Visualisations

The connection between visual selection (such as clicking or lasooing) and data ‘‘selection’’ has been
explored in the data visualisation literature, leading to various techniques for inverting selections in
visual space to obtain selections in the underlying data
[
26
,
19
]
. There is also a substantial
literature on linked brushing
[
4
,
22
]
as a way of connecting output selections via mediating
data selections. Although this has long been recognised as an important visual tool, with
[
33
]

arguing it should be ubiquitous, there is relatively little work on general-purpose infrastructure to support
it. For example Reactive Vega
[
34
]
provides a rich set of interactivity features to support
linked brushing, but no mechanism for automatically propagating selections through arbitrary intervening
queries; Glue
[
3
]
is a powerful Python library for building linked visualisations for scientific
applications, but the developer is responsible for specifying the relationships between data sets that unpin
the linking. More infrastructural approaches to linked brushing
[
26
,
22
,
31
]
,
similar in spirit to our work, have been developed mainly for relational languages. Relational languages are a
promising direction in data visualisation, but general-purpose languages continue to be widely used too, so it
is important to support linked brushing in this context as well.

6.5

Transparent research outputs

[
14
]
also develop techniques for transparent research outputs, via explorable multiverse
analysis, exposing
analysis parameters
, the methodological choices made in designing a data analysis.
Since different methodological choices will impact the conclusions of the analysis, they expose these choices
to users, and allow them to switch between different choices and observe the impact on the results. Our work
is potentially complementary: we are concerned with surfacing the dependencies that arise within a specific
choice of analysis parameters, whereas multiverse analyses are about exploring how things change under
different choices. It might be useful to package our dependency analysis as part of such a multiverse
analysis, allowing the user to observe the different dependency structures that arise from different
methodological decisions.

7

Conclusion and Future Work

Visualisations are an essential tool for communicating science and other data-driven claims, but can be hard
to make sense of and trust. Visualisations and other outputs that are ‘‘transparent’’ -- that can reveal to an
interested reader how they are related to data -- are more informative and trustworthy, but are also difficult
to produce. In this paper, we introduced a novel bidirectional program analysis framework for a
general-purpose programming language that makes it easier to create transparent visualisations by shifting
much of the burden to the language runtime. Relative to prior work based on execution traces and bidirectional
interpreters, our approach also makes life easier for the language implementor, by decomposing the system into
a single evaluator that builds a dependence graph, and a pair of language-agnostic bidirectional graph
algorithms. We also showed an overall performance improvement for provenance queries, at the cost of some
overhead in building the dependence graph.

7.1

Future work

We identify two important directions for future work. First, the dependency relation captured by the
dependence graph semantics in §
3
omits certain intuitively plausible edges, such as the

projection
edge that one might expect to exist between a record and the value of a contained field as a
consequence of evaluating a field access expression
\displaystyle e.x
. On the other hand, recording

all
structural dependencies of this nature would significantly bloat the dependence graph with
information that is only relevant in certain contexts (for example when one is specifically concerned with
where a value came from, rather than how it was computed). We would like to develop a more semantically
justified dependence graph, along with a proof that the graph is ‘‘complete’’ in some formal sense (cf. the

dependency correctness
notion of
[
9
]
), but anticipate that making this richer graph
practical will require distinguishing different kinds of query (e.g. ‘‘how’’ vs. ‘‘where from’’) for use in
different contexts.

Second, although the graph algorithms presented in §
4
compute graph slices as an interim data
structure, the internal nodes of the graph are eventually discarded. This is fine for the use cases in
§
2
, which only concern ‘‘extensional’’ (IO) transparency. However, intensional information
would be highly informative too: if highlighting a point in the moving average in
Figure
2
could show not only that three emissions inputs were relevant, but that
those values were summed and then divided by three, then the chart alone would serve as a full
‘‘self-explanatory’’ implementation of moving average.

This would connect to work on
executable program slicing

[
17
]
, and there are substantial
challenges that arise here as well: in particular, intensional explanations get potentially very large, so
ways of reducing and managing their complexity will be important. Again
[
9
]
offer inspiration:
their idea of
expression provenance
may be a way of presenting pared-back but still informative
explanations (for example showing the tree of primitive arithmetic operations that computed a value, but
omitting user-defined function calls and branches). And the user may only want intensional information for
certain subcomputations, and would be happy with just IO relationships for others --- in which case graph
transformations which elide internal nodes but preserve IO connectivity, such as the Y-
\displaystyle\Delta
transform

[
37
]
, may also have a role to play.

Lemma
1

For any relation
\displaystyle R\subseteq X\times Y
the functions
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{R}
and
\displaystyle\triangledown_{R}
are conjugate.

Proof

\displaystyle X^{\prime}\in\mathbb{P}(X)
,
\displaystyle Y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{P}(Y)
.

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{R}(X^{\prime})\cap Y^{\prime}=\varnothing\implies X^{\prime}\cap\triangledown_{R}(Y^{\prime})=\varnothing

\displaystyle\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{R}(X^{\prime})\cap Y^{\prime}=\varnothing

suppose

\displaystyle\displaystyle\{y\in Y\mid\exists x\in X^{\prime}.(x,y)\in R\}\cap Y^{\prime}=\varnothing

def of
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{R}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\{y\in Y^{\prime}\mid\exists x\in X^{\prime}.(x,y)\in R\}=\varnothing

def of
\displaystyle\cap

(1)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\forall y\in Y^{\prime}.\;\forall x\in X^{\prime}.(x,y)\not\in R

equivalent to (
1
)

(2)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\{x\in X^{\prime}\mid\exists y\in Y^{\prime}.(x,y)\in R\}=\varnothing

equivalent to (
2
)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{➪}\;X^{\prime}\cap\{x\in X\mid\exists y\in Y^{\prime}.(x,y)\in R\}=\varnothing

def of
\displaystyle\cap

\displaystyle\displaystyle X^{\prime}\cap\triangledown_{R}(Y^{\prime})=\varnothing

def of
\displaystyle\triangledown_{R}

WTS:

\displaystyle X^{\prime}\cap\triangledown_{R}(Y^{\prime})=\varnothing\implies\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{R}(X^{\prime})\cap Y^{\prime}=\varnothing

\displaystyle\displaystyle X^{\prime}\cap\triangledown_{R}(Y^{\prime})=\varnothing

suppose

\displaystyle\displaystyle X^{\prime}\cap\{x\in X\mid\exists y\in Y^{\prime}.(x,y)\in R\}=\varnothing

def of
\displaystyle\triangledown_{R}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{R}(X^{\prime})\cap Y^{\prime}=\varnothing

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{R}

0.A.2

Proposition
1

Suppose functions
\displaystyle f:A\to B
and
\displaystyle g:B\to A
between Boolean algebras. The following statements are
equivalent:

1.

\displaystyle f
and
\displaystyle g
form a conjugate pair

2.

\displaystyle f
and
\displaystyle g^{\circ}
form a Galois connection

Proof

Fix
\displaystyle x\in A
and
\displaystyle y\in B
.

WTS:
\displaystyle f,g
conjugate
\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle f,g^{\circ}
form Galois connection.

\displaystyle\displaystyle f(x)\leq_{B}y

(suppose)

\displaystyle\displaystyle f(x)\wedge_{B}\neg y=\bot

\displaystyle\displaystyle x\wedge_{A}g(\neg y)=\bot

(conjugacy)

\displaystyle\displaystyle x\leq_{A}\neg(g(\neg y))

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{➪}\;x\leq_{A}g^{\circ}(y)

(def of
\displaystyle\cdot^{\circ}
)

WTS:
\displaystyle f,g^{\circ}
Galois connection
\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle f,g
conjugate

\displaystyle\displaystyle f(x)\wedge_{B}y=\bot

suppose

\displaystyle\displaystyle f(x)\leq_{B}\neg y

\displaystyle\displaystyle x\leq_{A}g^{\circ}(\neg y)

(GC)

\displaystyle\displaystyle x\leq_{A}\neg(g(y))

(def of
\displaystyle\cdot^{\circ}
)

\displaystyle\displaystyle x\wedge_{A}g(y)=\bot

0.A.3

Lemma
2

Fix
\displaystyle R\subseteq X\times Y
. We proceed by direct calculation.

Proof

WTS:
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{R}^{\circ}=\blacktriangle_{R}
:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{R}(X^{\prime})^{\circ}=\{y\in Y\mid\exists x\in X^{\prime}.(x,y)\in R\}^{\circ}

def of
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{R}

0.A.5

Correctness of
\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{demByV}}}

The
output
relation of a graph
\displaystyle G
with reachability relation
\displaystyle R
, defined as
\displaystyle R\cap\textup{{V}}(G)\times\mathsf{T}(G)
, gives rise to the following extension of
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}
:

Definition 17
(
\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}^{*}
for a dependence graph)

\displaystyle G
with output relation
\displaystyle R
, define:

\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}^{*}_{G}:=\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{R}:\mathbb{P}(\textup{{V}}(G))\to\mathbb{P}(\textup{{V}}(G))

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}_{G}=\mathsf{T}(G)\cap\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}^{*}_{G}(X)
.

\displaystyle X\subseteq\mathsf{T}(G)

\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin]{180.0}{$\displaystyle\triangledown$}^{*}_{G}(X)=X
.

0.A.7

Correctness of
\displaystyle\mathrel{\textup{{suffE}}}

Definition 18
(Partial Slice)

Graphs
\displaystyle H,P,G
constitute a
partial slice
of the graph
\displaystyle G_{0}
iff
\displaystyle H,P,G
form a partition of
\displaystyle G_{0}
and:

(i)

\displaystyle\textup{{V}}(H)\subseteq\textup{{V}}(P)\subseteq\textup{{V}}(G)=\textup{{V}}(G_{0})

(ii)

\displaystyle\textup{{V}}(H)\supseteq\mathsf{T}(P)^{c}

(iii)

\displaystyle\textup{{V}}(H)=\mathsf{S}(P)

(iv)

\displaystyle\textup{{V}}(P)=\mathsf{S}(P)\cap\mathsf{T}(P)

(v)

\displaystyle\forall(\alpha,\beta)\in(\mathsf{S}(G_{0})\setminus\mathsf{S}(H))\times\textup{{V}}(H).\;(\alpha,\beta)\notin R_{G_{0}}

(vi)

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(G)\setminus(\mathsf{T}(P)\setminus\mathsf{S}(P))=\textup{{V}}(H)\uplus(\mathsf{S}(G_{0})\setminus\mathsf{S}(H))

Corollary 1

\displaystyle H,P,G
a partial slice
\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle H,P,G
edge disjoint and
\displaystyle\forall(\alpha,\beta)\in\textup{{E}}(G)\cup\textup{{E}}(P).\;\beta\notin\textup{{V}}(H)

Definition 19
(Input Relation)

For a graph
\displaystyle G
with reachability relation
\displaystyle R
, define it’s
input relation

\displaystyle R_{I}:=(\mathsf{S}(G)\times\textup{{V}}(G))\cap R

Definition 20
(
\displaystyle\blacktriangle^{*}
for a dependence graph)

For a graph
\displaystyle G
, with an input relation
\displaystyle R_{I}
, and a set of input vertices
\displaystyle X\subseteq\mathsf{S}(G)
:

\blacktriangle^{*}_{G}(X):=\blacktriangle_{R_{I}}(X)=\{y\in\textup{{V}}(G)\mid\nexists x\in\mathsf{S}(G)\setminus X.\;(x,y)\in R_{I}\}

Lemma 7

\displaystyle\blacktriangle_{G}(X)=\mathsf{T}(G)\cap\blacktriangle^{*}_{G}(X)

Lemma 8

If
\displaystyle H,P,G
a partial slice of
\displaystyle G_{0}
with
\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(G)\cap\textup{{V}}(P)\subseteq\mathsf{S}(P)

\displaystyle\textup{{V}}(H)\subseteq\mathsf{T}(G)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\textup{{V}}(H)\subseteq\mathsf{T}(G)

(2)

direction

,

Lemma 9

[1]

1.

If
\displaystyle\alpha\in\textup{{V}}(H)
,
\displaystyle E=\textup{{outE}}_{G}(\alpha)\neq\varnothing
then:

\displaystyle H,P,G
a partial slice of
\displaystyle G_{0}

\displaystyle\iff H,P\cup E,G\setminus_{\mathsf{E}}E
a partial slice of
\displaystyle G_{0}

2.

\displaystyle\alpha\in\mathsf{S}(G)
,
\displaystyle E=\textup{{inE}}_{P}(\alpha)\neq\varnothing
then:
\displaystyle H,P,G
a partial slice
\displaystyle G_{0}

\displaystyle\iff H\cup E,P\setminus_{\mathsf{E}}E,G
a partial slice of
\displaystyle G_{0}

Proof

(1.II),
\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\displaystyle E=\{\alpha\}\times B

suppose

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathsf{T}(P)^{c}\subseteq\textup{{V}}(H)

suppose

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathsf{T}(P\cup E)^{c}=\mathsf{T}(P)^{c}\cup\{\alpha\}

def.
\displaystyle E

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{➪}\;\mathsf{T}(P\cup E)^{c}\subseteq\textup{{V}}(H)

\displaystyle\alpha\in\textup{{V}}(H)

(1.II),
\displaystyle\Leftarrow

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathsf{T}(P\cup E)^{c}\subseteq\textup{{V}}(H)

suppose

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathsf{T}(P)^{c}\subseteq\mathsf{T}(P\cup E)^{c}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{➪}\;\mathsf{T}(P)^{c}\subseteq\textup{{V}}(H)

(1.III),
\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\displaystyle E=\{\alpha\}\times{B}

suppose

\displaystyle\displaystyle\textup{{V}}(H)=\mathsf{S}(P)

suppose

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{Need to show: }\textup{{V}}(H)=\mathsf{S}(P\cup E)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(P\cup E)=\mathsf{S}(P)\setminus(\mathsf{S}(P)\cap B)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{Need to show: }\mathsf{S}(P)\cap B=\varnothing

\displaystyle\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta)\in\textup{{E}}(G)

\displaystyle\forall(\alpha,\beta)\in E

\displaystyle\displaystyle\beta\in\mathsf{S}(P)=\textup{{V}}(H)\implies\bot

Contra partial slice(V)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{➪}\;\beta\notin\mathsf{S}(G)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{➪}\;\mathsf{S}(P)\cap\beta=\varnothing

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{➪}\;\textup{{V}}(H)=\mathsf{S}(P\cup E)

(1.III),
\displaystyle\Leftarrow

\displaystyle\displaystyle\textup{{V}}(H)=\mathsf{S}(P\cup E)

suppose

\displaystyle\displaystyle E=\{\alpha\}\times B

suppose

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{Want to show: }\textup{{V}}(H)=\mathsf{S}(P)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(P)=\mathsf{S}(P\cup E)\setminus B

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{suffices to show: }\mathsf{S}(P\cup E)\cap B=\varnothing

\displaystyle\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta)\in E.\;(\alpha,\beta)\in\textup{{E}}(G)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{➪}\;\beta\notin\textup{{V}}(H)=\mathsf{S}(P\cup E)

partial slice (V)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{➪}\;\mathsf{S}(P\cup E)\cap B=\varnothing

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{➪}\;\mathsf{S}(P)=\mathsf{S}(P\cup E)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{➪}\;\textup{{V}}(H)=\mathsf{S}(P)

(1.IV),
\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\displaystyle\textup{{V}}(P)=\mathsf{S}(P)\cup\mathsf{T}(P)

suppose

\displaystyle\displaystyle E=B\times\{\alpha\}

suppose

\displaystyle\displaystyle\textup{{V}}(P\cup E)=\textup{{V}}(P)\cup B

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{suffices to show that:}\mathsf{S}(P)\cup\mathsf{T}(P)\cup B=\mathsf{S}(P\cup E)\cup\mathsf{T}(P\cup E)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(P)=\mathsf{S}(P\cup E)

proved in proof of (iii)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\alpha\in\mathsf{S}(P)=\mathsf{S}(P\cup E)

\displaystyle\alpha\in\textup{{V}}(H)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathsf{T}(P\cup E)=(\mathsf{T}(P)\cup B)\setminus{\alpha}

def.
\displaystyle E

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{➪}\;\mathsf{S}(P)\cup\mathsf{T}(P)\cup B=\mathsf{S}(P\cup E)\cup((\mathsf{T}(P)\cup B)\setminus\{\alpha\})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{➪}\;\mathsf{S}(P)\cup\mathsf{T}(P)\cup B=\mathsf{S}(P\cup E)\cup\mathsf{T}(P\cup E)

(1.IV),
\displaystyle\Leftarrow

\displaystyle\displaystyle\textup{{V}}(P\cup E)=\mathsf{S}(P\cup E)\cup\mathsf{T}(P\cup E)

suppose

\displaystyle\displaystyle\textup{{V}}(P\cup E)=\textup{{V}}(P)\cup B

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(P\cup E)\cup\mathsf{T}(P\cup E)=\textup{{V}}(P)\cup B

def.
\displaystyle E

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathsf{T}(P\cup E)=(\mathsf{T}(P)\cup B)\setminus\{\alpha\}

def.
\displaystyle E

\displaystyle\displaystyle B\cap\mathsf{S}(P)=\varnothing

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(P)\setminus B=\mathsf{S}(P)

\displaystyle\displaystyle(\mathsf{S}(P\cup E)\cup\mathsf{T}(P\cup E))\setminus B=\textup{{V}}(P)

\displaystyle\displaystyle(\mathsf{S}(P\cup E)\setminus B)\cup((\mathsf{T}(P)\cup B\setminus\{\alpha\})\setminus B)=\textup{{V}}(P)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{➪}\;\mathsf{S}(P)\cup(\mathsf{T}(P)\setminus\{\alpha\})=\textup{{V}}(P)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{➪}\;\mathsf{S}(P)\cup\mathsf{T}(P)=\textup{{V}}(P)

\displaystyle\alpha\in\mathsf{S}(P)

(1.V),
\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\displaystyle H,P,G\text{ a partial slice}

suppose

\displaystyle\Leftarrow

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{Suffices to show: }\mathsf{S}(G)\setminus(\mathsf{T}(P)\setminus\mathsf{S}(P))=\mathsf{S}(G\setminus_{\mathsf{E}}E)\setminus(\mathsf{T}(P\cup E)\setminus\mathsf{S}(P\cup E))

suppose

\displaystyle\displaystyle E=\{\alpha\}\times B

\displaystyle\displaystyle\textbf{\emph{Subcase}}\ x\in\mathsf{S}(G)\setminus(\mathsf{T}(P)\setminus\mathsf{S}(P)).

\displaystyle\displaystyle x\in\mathsf{S}(G)\subseteq\mathsf{S}(G\setminus_{\mathsf{E}}E)

\displaystyle\displaystyle x\notin\mathsf{T}(P)\setminus\mathsf{S}(P)

(3)

\displaystyle\displaystyle x\notin B

\displaystyle x\in\mathsf{S}(G)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{➪}\;x\notin\mathsf{T}(P\cup E)\setminus\mathsf{S}(P\cup E)

\displaystyle\displaystyle x\in\mathsf{S}(G\setminus_{\mathsf{E}}E)\setminus(\mathsf{T}(P\cup E)\setminus\mathsf{S}(P\cup E))

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{➪}\;\mathsf{S}(G)\setminus{\mathsf{T}(P)\setminus\mathsf{S}(P)}\subseteq\mathsf{S}(G\setminus_{\mathsf{E}}E)\setminus(\mathsf{T}(P\cup E)\setminus\mathsf{S}(P\cup E))

(4)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\textbf{\emph{Subcase}}\ x\notin\mathsf{S}(G)\setminus(\mathsf{T}(P)\setminus\mathsf{S}(P)).

\displaystyle\displaystyle\textbf{\emph{Subcase}}\ x\in\mathsf{S}(G).

\displaystyle\displaystyle x\in\mathsf{T}(P)\setminus\mathsf{S}(P)

(5)

\displaystyle\displaystyle x\in\mathsf{T}(P\cup E)\setminus\mathsf{S}(P\cup E)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{➪}\;x\notin\mathsf{S}(G\setminus_{\mathsf{E}}E)\setminus(\mathsf{T}(P\cup E)\setminus\mathsf{S}(P\cup E))

\displaystyle\displaystyle\textbf{\emph{Subcase}}\ x\notin\mathsf{S}(G)\wedge x\in\mathsf{S}(G\setminus_{\mathsf{E}}E).

\displaystyle\displaystyle x\notin\mathsf{S}(G)\setminus(\mathsf{T}(P)\setminus\mathsf{S}(P))

\displaystyle\displaystyle(\alpha,x)\in E

\displaystyle x\in\mathsf{S}(G\setminus_{\mathsf{E}}E)\setminus\mathsf{S}(G)

(6)

\displaystyle\displaystyle x\in(\mathsf{T}(P\cup E)\setminus\mathsf{S}(P\cup E))

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{➪}\;x\notin\mathsf{S}(G\setminus_{\mathsf{E}}E)\setminus(\mathsf{T}(P\cup E)\setminus\mathsf{S}(P\cup E))

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{➪}\;\mathsf{S}(G\setminus_{\mathsf{E}}E)\setminus(\mathsf{T}(P\cup E)\setminus\mathsf{S}(P\cup E))\subseteq\mathsf{S}(G)\setminus(\mathsf{T}(P)\setminus\mathsf{S}(P))

(7)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{➪}\;\mathsf{S}(G)\setminus{\mathsf{T}(P)\setminus\mathsf{S}(P)}=\mathsf{S}(G\setminus_{\mathsf{E}}E)\setminus(\mathsf{T}(P\cup E)\setminus\mathsf{S}(P\cup E))

(2.II),
\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\displaystyle E=B\times\{\alpha\}

suppose

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathsf{T}(P)^{c}\subseteq\textup{{V}}(H)

suppose

\displaystyle\displaystyle\textup{{V}}(H\cup E)=\textup{{V}}(H)\cup\{\alpha\}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{➪}\;\mathsf{T}(P\setminus_{\mathsf{E}}E)^{c}\subseteq\textup{{V}}(H)\cup\{\alpha\}

(2.II),
\displaystyle\Leftarrow

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathsf{T}(P\setminus_{\mathsf{E}}E)^{c}\subseteq\textup{{V}}(H\cup E)

suppose

\displaystyle\displaystyle\forall\beta\in B.\;\beta\in\textup{{V}}(H)\cup\{\alpha\}

suppose

straightforward

suppose

\displaystyle\displaystyle x\in\textup{{V}}(P)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(P)\cup\mathsf{T}(P)\subseteq\mathsf{S}(P\setminus_{\mathsf{E}}E)\cup\mathsf{T}(P\setminus_{\mathsf{E}}E)

Lemma 10

Suppose
\displaystyle H,P,G
a partial slice of
\displaystyle G_{0}
, then:

1.

\displaystyle\blacktriangle^{*}_{G\cup P\cup H}(\mathsf{S}(H))=\blacktriangle^{*}_{(G\setminus_{\mathsf{E}}E)\cup(P\cup E)\cup H}(\mathsf{S}(H))

2.

\displaystyle\blacktriangle^{*}_{G\cup P\cup H}(\mathsf{S}(H))=\blacktriangle^{*}_{G\cup(P\setminus_{\mathsf{E}}E)\cup(H\cup E)}(\mathsf{S}(H\cup E))

Proof

1.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}G_{0}=}\;G\cup P\cup H=(G\setminus_{\mathsf{E}}E)\cup(P\cup E)\cup H

2.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}G_{0}=}\;G\cup P\cup H=G\cup(P\setminus_{\mathsf{E}}E)\cup(H\cup E)
, and
\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(H)=\mathsf{S}(H\cup E)

Proposition 5

\displaystyle H,P,G

\displaystyle G_{0}
:

(\exists H^{\prime}.\;H,P,G\mathrel{\textup{{suffE}}}H^{\prime}\text{ with }\textup{{V}}(H^{\prime})=Y)\iff\blacktriangle^{*}_{G_{0}}(\mathsf{S}(H))=Y
